We calculate leading resonant widths and local homoclinic splitting, and derive a damping-dependent splitting threshold for a stated scalar reduction. These results identify analytical structures relevant to transport and explain possible polarization-based electrical readout. Establishing Arnold diffusion in the coupled model requires additional connecting-orbit analysis or controlled long-time evidence; neither a diffusion law nor Nekhoroshev stability of the driven model is inferred from the present finite-time calculations.

</details>


### [163] [Supersymmetric Neuromorphic FitzHugh--Nagumo model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03312)
*D. Maroulakos,A. Wal,S. K. Mishra,Z. Toklikishvili,L. Chotorlishvili*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a supersymmetric spectral approach to analyze stochastic FitzHugh–Nagumo dynamics inspired by an antiferromagnetic neuromorphic model; it derives exact and approximate relaxation eigenvalues, introduces a normalized two-state amplitude for coherence quantification, and clarifies the domain of validity of this localized-basis coherence diagnostic—particularly distinguishing classical stochastic coherence from quantum coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge stochastic neurodynamics with supersymmetric quantum-mechanical spectral methods, motivated by an effective antiferromagnetic neuromorphic model where tangential Néel-vector dynamics map onto FitzHugh–Nagumo variables; specifically, to quantify and delimit a classical coherence diagnostic grounded in occupation probabilities rather than quantum superposition.

Method: Performs local normal-form reduction of Néel-vector dynamics to obtain fast/slow variables; freezes recovery to obtain a 1D Fokker–Planck equation with quartic potential; applies similarity transformation and exact supersymmetric factorization of its generator; computes leading relaxation eigenvalue via partner Hamiltonian ground state; approximates it using Kramers interwell rates in weak-noise bistable regime; defines a normalized auxiliary two-state amplitude via projection onto lowest eigenmodes; derives its localized-basis coherence in terms of time-dependent basin populations and equilibrium weights.

Result: The first nonzero relaxation eigenvalue is exactly determined via supersymmetry and approximated by sum of Kramers rates; the auxiliary two-state amplitude’s coherence peaks when normalized amplitudes are equal (not necessarily basin populations); a pronounced coherence pulse arises in fixed-recovery relaxation without spiking; deterministic spiking violates the bistability assumption, limiting validity of the two-state approximation.

Conclusion: Stochastic relaxation spectra can be rigorously linked to an occupation-based coherence diagnostic via supersymmetric spectral theory; however, this diagnostic reflects classical stochastic structure—not microscopic quantum coherence—and its applicability is constrained to parameter regimes where the bistable, well-separated-well approximation holds, e.g., excluding spiking trajectories that cross low-barrier regions.

Abstract: We develop a supersymmetric spectral description of stochastic FitzHugh--Nagumo dynamics motivated by an effective antiferromagnetic neuromorphic model. A local normal-form reduction of the adopted tangential Néel-vector dynamics relates magnetic control parameters to fast activation and slow recovery coordinates. Freezing the recovery variable yields a one-dimensional Fokker--Planck equation with a quartic potential, whose similarity-transformed generator admits an exact supersymmetric factorization. The first nonzero relaxation eigenvalue is determined by the ground state of the partner operator; in the weak-noise bistable regime, it is approximated by the sum of the two interwell Kramers rates. Projecting the similarity-transformed probability density onto the two lowest modes defines a normalized auxiliary two-state amplitude. We derive its localized-basis coherence in terms of both the time-dependent basin populations and their equilibrium weights. Its maximum corresponds to equal normalized amplitude weights, which generally differ from equal basin populations. The fixed-recovery example exhibits a pronounced coherence pulse during monotonic relaxation without a spike. Conversely, the illustrated deterministic spiking trajectory repeatedly leaves the bistable domain and passes through regions with insufficient barrier separation, restricting the validity of the localized two-state approximation. These results connect stochastic relaxation spectra to an occupation-based coherence diagnostic and delimit its application to neuromorphic spiking. The diagnostic characterizes an auxiliary amplitude representation of classical stochastic dynamics and does not by itself establish microscopic quantum coherence.

</details>


### [164] [Lossless propagation bounds for long-range fermionic interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03441)
*Stefan Teufel*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes optimal propagation estimates for fermionic many-body interactions under time evolution, proving polynomial-in-time growth of local interaction norms without loss of spatial decay, with a sharp threshold a > d_vol for the spatial exponent; it extends to spin systems and enables rigorous adiabatic, perturbative, and response-theoretic analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To obtain rigorous, quantitative bounds on how fermionic many-body interactions spread under time evolution—specifically, to achieve uniform polynomial time growth, preserve spatial decay, and identify the optimal spatial exponent threshold—enabling robust applications in adiabatic theory, quantum belief propagation, and response theory.

Method: Constructs local representatives of evolved interactions on metric spaces with polynomial volume growth d_vol using weighted interaction norms (e^{κ|X|}(1+diam X)^a/p); employs Lieb–Robinson–type techniques adapted to extensive fermionic Hamiltonians and filtered dynamics; proves optimality of the threshold a > d_vol via counterexamples for a ≤ d_vol.

Result: Local interaction norms of evolved interactions grow at most polynomially in |t−s| with weight e^{c(1+|t−s|)^{−b}|X|}(1+diam X)^p, allowing p = a (no faster decay required for Hamiltonian); threshold a > d_vol is sharp; results extend to spin systems; enables dynamical estimates for gap stability, LPPL, adiabatic evolution, and linear response.

Conclusion: The derived propagation estimates are optimal in spatial exponent, generalizable to spin systems, and provide a foundational framework for rigorous analysis of quasi-adiabatic continuation, perturbation theory with extensive terms, and locality-preserving dynamics in quantum many-body systems.

Abstract: We prove propagation estimates for extensive fermionic many-body interactions with polynomial time growth, no loss of spatial decay, and an optimal threshold for the polynomial spatial exponent. More precisely, we construct local representatives of evolved fermionic many-body interactions on metric spaces with polynomial volume growth of order $d_{\mathrm{vol}}$ and suitable cell partitions. For the even Hamiltonian interaction $\Phi_u$ and the even interaction $\Psi$ being evolved, we assume bounds, uniform in the site, on sums of term norms over finite supports $X$ containing a given site, with weights $e^{\kappa|X|}(1+\operatorname{diam}X)^a$ and $e^{\kappa|X|}(1+\operatorname{diam}X)^p$, respectively. Here $a>d_{\mathrm{vol}}$, $0\le p\le a$, $\kappa$ is sufficiently large, and the Hamiltonian bound is uniform in time. We construct local representatives whose interaction norms with weight $e^{c(1+|t-s|)^{-b}|X|}(1+\operatorname{diam}X)^p$ grow at most polynomially in $|t-s|$, for suitable $c,b>0$. In particular, $p=a$ is allowed: the Hamiltonian need not decay faster than the interaction being evolved. The threshold $a>d_{\mathrm{vol}}$ is optimal in general: uniform polynomial time bounds at $p=a$ fail for $0<a\le d_{\mathrm{vol}}$, even for smooth time-dependent two-site interactions. Polynomial time growth permits integration against filters with sufficiently many finite absolute moments, including those used in quasi-adiabatic constructions and quantum belief propagation. We derive consequences for filtered interactions and the dynamics they generate, providing dynamical estimates for perturbation theory with extensive perturbations, including gap stability, the principle that local perturbations perturb locally (LPPL), adiabatic theory, and response theory. All results also hold for quantum spin systems.

</details>


### [165] [Koopman-von Neumann formalism meets contact Hamiltonian systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03522)
*Federico Zadra*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper formulates contact Hamiltonian dynamics in the Koopman–von Neumann (KvN) framework on a Hilbert space built from the contact volume, ensuring unitarity via a divergence correction; it proves symmetry, essential self-adjointness for complete flows, covariance under contactomorphisms, and establishes invariant decompositions and links to symplectization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the Koopman–von Neumann formalism—traditionally used for conservative (symplectic) classical mechanics—to dissipative or non-conservative systems governed by contact Hamiltonian dynamics, while preserving key quantum-like structural properties such as unitarity and self-adjointness.

Method: Constructs a KvN Hilbert space using the contact volume form; derives the KvN generator with explicit divergence correction to ensure skew-adjointness; applies functional-analytic tools (e.g., essential self-adjointness criteria, spectral theory of unbounded operators) and contact geometry (contactomorphisms, symplectization) to analyze evolution, symmetries, and integrability.

Result: The KvN generator is skew-adjoint (hence unitary evolution), symmetric, and essentially self-adjoint for complete contact Hamiltonian vector fields; it transforms covariantly under contactomorphisms; for completely integrable systems, admits an invariant direct-integral decomposition with strongly commuting generators; and is shown to be naturally related to the KvN dynamics of the symplectized system.

Conclusion: The KvN framework can be rigorously extended to contact Hamiltonian systems, preserving core operator-theoretic and geometric structures—unitarity, symmetry, covariance, and integrability—thereby providing a unified Hilbert-space-based description bridging contact geometry and classical statistical mechanics.

Abstract: The present work develops a Koopman--von Neumann (KvN) formulation of contact Hamiltonian dynamics on the Hilbert space defined by the contact volume. The resulting generator includes the divergence correction required for unitary evolution. We establish symmetry and, for complete contact Hamiltonian vector fields, essential self-adjointness and a strongly continuous unitary evolution; we also describe covariance under contactomorphisms. For completely integrable contact systems with complete flows, it is possible to recover an invariant direct-integral decomposition and strongly commuting KvN generators. We further relate contact KvN dynamics to the KvN formulation of its symplectization.

</details>

# hep-ex [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [LH$μ$C Concept: Energy-Frontier Antimuon-Proton Collisions at CERN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01117)
*Arda Goroglu,Saleh Sultansoy*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: Proposes the LHμC, a novel antimuon-proton collider leveraging HL-LHC infrastructure to achieve √s = 5.3 TeV and high luminosity; compares muon-beam implementation strategies, outlines detector design and physics reach (e.g., colour-octet muons, RPV squarks), and identifies key technical challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy-frontier lepton-hadron colliders are essential for precision QCD studies and BSM physics exploration; existing proposals lack optimal energy–luminosity balance, motivating a new μp collider based on HL-LHC infrastructure.

Method: Compares two μp collision schemes at LHC: (i) muon-collider beams, and (ii) ultra-cold μ⁺ beams à la μTRISTAN, using a μTRISTAN-type booster and repurposed LHeC ERL tunnel; performs luminosity and center-of-mass energy calculations; outlines detector concept and physics benchmark analyses.

Result: Achieves √s = 5.3 TeV with 1 TeV antimuons and 7 TeV protons; peak luminosities of 8.8×10³³ and 7.2×10³³ cm⁻²s⁻¹ for two configurations; identifies viable physics channels including colour-octet muons and R-parity-violating squark searches.

Conclusion: The LHμC is a technically feasible, high-physics-potential extension of HL-LHC, offering unique QCD and BSM sensitivity; realization hinges on overcoming muon beam cooling, acceleration, and background mitigation challenges.

Abstract: The concept of the LH$\mu$C, an energy-frontier antimuon-proton collider based on the HL-LHC, is presented. After recalling why energy-frontier lepton-hadron colliders are indispensable as QCD explorers and reviewing the history of muon-proton collider proposals, two ways of realising $\mu p$ collisions at the LHC are compared: with muon-collider beams and with ultra-cold $\mu^+$ beams as proposed for $\mu$TRISTAN. For the latter, a $\mu$TRISTAN-type booster and the re-use of the LHeC energy-recovery-linac tunnel are discussed; colliding 1 TeV antimuons with 7 TeV protons gives $\sqrt{s}=5.3$ TeV and peak luminosities of $8.8\times10^{33}$ and $7.2\times10^{33}$ cm$^{-2}$s$^{-1}$, respectively. The detector concept and the physics potential, including searches for colour-octet muons and R-parity-violating squarks, are discussed together with the main challenges.

</details>


### [167] [Reinforcement Learning in High-Energy Physics: A Survey from Accelerator Control to Theory Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02049)
*Luca Anzalone*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This survey reviews reinforcement learning (RL) applications in high-energy physics (HEP), covering accelerator control, tracking, jet grooming, detector design, BSM model building, and more; it compares RL methods (model-free, model-based, multi-agent, offline, inverse, bandit) against traditional approaches, highlights RL’s advantages in closed-loop, simulator-backed, reward-driven, sequential decision tasks, and identifies recurring challenges: reward design, sim-to-real transfer, safety, benchmark scarcity, and statistical validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many HEP tasks are inherently closed-loop—data depend on agent actions, feedback is sparse/delayed, and objectives are evaluated on real hardware or stochastic simulations—not fixed datasets—making supervised/unsupervised learning inadequate and RL a natural fit.

Method: The paper surveys RL methodologies including model-free (e.g., DQN, PPO), model-based, multi-agent, offline, inverse, and bandit RL; contextualizes them within HEP domains; and critically compares them to classical methods (e.g., Kalman filters, clustering heuristics, Bayesian optimization, genetic algorithms).

Result: RL has been successfully deployed in real accelerator control and data quality monitoring, used for jet clustering/grooming and charged particle tracking, applied to BSM/string model search and detector optimization, and explored in formal QFT computation; head-to-head comparisons show RL excels in fast-simulation-reliant, sequential, fault-resilient, reward-defined tasks but faces limitations in statistical rigor and sim-to-real fidelity.

Conclusion: RL is a powerful and increasingly operational paradigm for closed-loop HEP problems, yet widespread adoption is hindered by persistent challenges: principled reward engineering, robust sim-to-real transfer, safety guarantees, absence of standardized benchmarks, and ensuring statistically valid, policy-driven inference.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is a machine learning paradigm in which an agent learns a decision-making policy by interacting with an environment, guided by a scalar reward rather than explicit supervision. Supervised and unsupervised learning have become indispensable tools in high-energy physics (HEP), but they learn from fixed datasets, whereas many HEP tasks are closed-loop decisions: the data an algorithm observes depend on its choices, the feedback is sparse or delayed, and the objective is often measured on a real machine or in a stochastic simulation, not by a differentiable model. This survey reviews the application of RL to such tasks: accelerator control and feedback, data quality monitoring, jet grooming, clustering, and representation, charged particle tracking, beyond-the-Standard-Model (BSM) and string model building, detector design, trigger control, analysis, and formal quantum field theory computation. It provides a self-contained introduction to the underlying RL methods, from model-free and model-based to multi-agent, offline, inverse, and bandit approaches, reviews each domain against its traditional methods, and summarizes the head-to-head comparisons. The applications range from policies deployed on real accelerators and evaluated on recorded collision data to exploratory studies in formal theory. RL is most advantageous for repeated tasks with a fast simulator, decisions inside fast control loops, operation under faults and corrupted measurements, physics-based rewards in place of labels, and sequential discrete constructions with a reusable policy, while Bayesian optimization, genetic algorithms, and differentiable methods remain competitive elsewhere. We conclude with the open challenges that recur across domains, including reward design, sim-to-real transfer, safety, the lack of shared benchmarks, and the statistical validity of policy-driven decisions.

</details>


### [168] [Search for $\mathrm{GeV}$-scale axion-like particles in PbPb collisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00135)
*LHCb collaboration,R. Aaij,M. Abdelfatah,A.S.W. Abdelmotteleb,C. Abellan Beteta,F. Abudinén,T. Ackernley,A.A. Adefisoye,B. Adeva,M. Adinolfi,P. Adlarson,C. Agapopoulou,C.A. Aidala,S. Akar,K. Akiba,H. Al Saleh,P. Albicocco,J. Albrecht,R. Aleksiejunas,F. Alessio,P. Alvarez Cartelle,S. Amato,J.L. Amey,Y. Amhis,Z. Amos,L. An,L. Anderlini,P. Andreola,M. Andreotti,S. Andres Estrada,A. Anelli,D. Ao,C. Arata,F. Archilli,Z. Areg,M. Argenton,S. Arguedas Cuendis,L. Arnone,M. Artuso,E. Aslanides,R. Ataíde Da Silva,M. Atzeni,B. Audurier,J.A. Authier,D. Bacher,I. Bachiller Perea,S. Bachmann,M. Bachmayer,J.J. Back,M. Bai,Z.B. Bai,V. Balagura,A. Balboni,W. Baldini,Z. Baldwin,L. Balzani,H. Bao,J. Baptista de Souza Leite,C. Barbero Pretel,M. Barbetti,I.R. Barbosa,W. Barker,R.J. Barlow,M. Barnyakov,S. Baron,S. Barsuk,W. Barter,J. Bartz,S. Bashir,B. Batsukh,P.B. Battista,A. Bavarchee,A. Bay,A. Beck,M. Becker,F. Bedeschi,I.B. Bediaga,N.A. Behling,S. Belin,A. Bellavista,I. Belyaev,G. Bencivenni,E. Ben-Haim,J.L.M. Berkey,R. Bernet,A. Bertolin,L. Bertsch,F. Betti,J. Bex,O. Bezshyyko,S. Bhattacharya,M.S. Bieker,N.V. Biesuz,A. Biolchini,M. Birch,F.C.R. Bishop,A. Bitadze,A. Bizzeti,T. Blake,F. Blanc*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: First LHCb search for axion-like particles (ALPs) in PbPb collisions via exclusive γγ → a → γγ in the forward region; sets strongest limits for 3–5 GeV ALP masses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To probe unexplored regions of axion-like particle (ALP) parameter space—specifically low masses (3–10 GeV) and couplings—via exclusive two-photon fusion in heavy-ion collisions, where coherent photon fluxes enhance sensitivity.

Method: Analyzed 0.228 nb⁻¹ of PbPb collision data at √s_NN = 5.02 TeV with LHCb; searched for exclusive diphoton resonances in the forward region, reconstructing ALPs from γγ → a → γγ with full event topology and background suppression using kinematic vetoes and isolation criteria.

Result: No significant excess observed; set upper limits on ALP production cross-section and coupling g_aγγ down to 3.2×10⁻⁴ GeV⁻¹ for m_a = 3.0 GeV; most stringent limits to date for m_a ∈ [3, 5] GeV.

Conclusion: Excludes previously unprobed ALP parameter space; demonstrates feasibility of forward-exclusive γγ fusion searches in heavy-ion collisions at LHCb; constrains ALP models coupled to photons in the few-GeV mass range.

Abstract: A search is performed for the production of axion-like particles with masses in the range 3-10 GeV using PbPb collision data recorded by the LHCb experiment, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of $0.228~\mathrm{nb}^{-1}$ and a nucleon-nucleon centre-of-mass energy of $\sqrt{s_{\mathrm{NN}}}=5.02$ TeV. This is the first search for axion-like particles produced exclusively by two photons ($\gamma\gamma\to a\to\gamma\gamma$) in the forward direction. It extends sensitivity to previously unexplored regions of the parameter space, probing axion-like particle masses and couplings down to 3.0 GeV and $3.2\times10^{-4}~\mathrm{GeV}^{-1}$, respectively. This result provides the most stringent limits to date on the production of axion-like particles from exclusive photon-photon fusion processes with masses between 3 and 5 GeV.

</details>


### [169] [Evidence for the decay $B^0 \to μ^+ μ^-$ and improved measurements of the decay $B^0_s \to μ^+ μ^-$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00230)
*LHCb collaboration,R. Aaij,M. Abdelfatah,A.S.W. Abdelmotteleb,C. Abellan Beteta,F. Abudinén,T. Ackernley,A.A. Adefisoye,B. Adeva,M. Adinolfi,P. Adlarson,C. Agapopoulou,L. Ahrens,C.A. Aidala,S. Akar,K. Akiba,H. Al Saleh,P. Albicocco,J. Albrecht,R. Aleksiejunas,F. Alessio,P. Alvarez Cartelle,A.A. Alves Jr,S. Amato,Y. Amhis,Z. Amos,L. An,L. Anderlini,P. Andreola,M. Andreotti,S. Andres Estrada,A. Anelli,C. Arata,F. Archilli,Z. Areg,M. Argenton,S. Arguedas Cuendis,L. Arnone,M. Artuso,E. Aslanides,R. Ataíde Da Silva,M. Atzeni,B. Audurier,J.A. Authier,D. Bacher,I. Bachiller Perea,S. Bachmann,J.J. Back,M. Bai,Z.B. Bai,V. Balagura,A. Balboni,W. Baldini,L. Balzani,H. Bao,J. Baptista de Souza Leite,C. Barbero Pretel,I.R. Barbosa,W. Barker,R.J. Barlow,M. Barnyakov,S. Baron,S. Barsuk,W. Barter,J. Bartz,S. Bashir,B. Batsukh,P.B. Battista,A. Bavarchee,A. Bay,A. Beck,M. Becker,F. Bedeschi,I.B. Bediaga,N.A. Behling,S. Belin,A. Bellavista,I. Belyaev,G. Bencivenni,E. Ben-Haim,J.L.M. Berkey,R. Bernet,A. Bertolin,L. Bertsch,F. Betti,J. Bex,O. Bezshyyko,S. Bhattacharya,M.S. Bieker,N.V. Biesuz,A. Biolchini,M. Birch,F.C.R. Bishop,A. Bitadze,A. Bizzeti,F. Blanc,J.E. Blank,S. Blusk,J.A. Boelhauve,O. Boente Garcia*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: First single-experiment observation of B⁰ → μ⁺μ⁻; most precise B⁰ₛ → μ⁺μ⁻ branching fraction and effective lifetime measurements using LHCb Upgrade I Run 3 data (18 fb⁻¹ at 13.6 TeV); results consistent with Standard Model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test lepton-flavor universality and probe new physics via rare flavor-changing neutral current decays, which are highly suppressed in the Standard Model and thus sensitive to beyond-Standard-Model contributions.

Method: Analysis of proton-proton collision data from LHCb Upgrade I detector during Run 3 (2024–2025, √s = 13.6 TeV, 18 fb⁻¹), combined with previous Run 1 and Run 2 LHCb results; signal extraction via unbinned maximum-likelihood fit to dimuon invariant mass and decay-time distributions.

Result: First single-experiment evidence for B⁰ → μ⁺μ⁻ (3.4σ); ℬ(B⁰ₛ → μ⁺μ⁻) = (3.45 ± 0.29) × 10⁻⁹; τ_eff(B⁰ₛ → μ⁺μ⁻) = 1.75 ± 0.12 ps; all consistent with SM predictions.

Conclusion: No significant deviation from Standard Model predictions is observed, constraining new physics parameter space—particularly models with extended Higgs sectors or supersymmetry—that enhance B⁰ₛ/B⁰ → μ⁺μ⁻ rates.

Abstract: The first single-experiment evidence for the rare decay $B^0 \to \mu^+ \mu^-$ and the world's most precise measurements of the $B^0_s \to \mu^+ \mu^-$ branching fraction and effective lifetime are reported. The results are obtained using Run 3 proton-proton collision data corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 18 fb$^{-1}$ recorded with the LHCb Upgrade I detector at a centre-of-mass energy of $13.6$ TeV between 2024 and 2025. Their combination with the Run 1 and Run 2 LHCb results further improves the precision. All measurements are found to be in agreement with the Standard Model expectations.

</details>


### [170] [Heavy Quarkonium Physics: An Experimental Review at Tevatron, LHC, and e+e- Colliders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00948)
*Zhipeng Cui,Yilin Zhou,Gerry Bauer,Kai Yi*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reviews recent experimental advances in heavy quarkonium physics across multiple colliders (Tevatron, e+e− facilities, LHC), focusing on differential cross sections, polarization, spectroscopy, exotic states, QGP probes, and multi-quarkonium production — collectively establishing quarkonium as a key probe of QCD and BSM physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To synthesize and interpret recent experimental results on heavy quarkonium (charmonium and bottomonium) across collider environments, assess their implications for perturbative and non-perturbative QCD—particularly within the NRQCD framework—and evaluate their sensitivity to QGP formation, cold nuclear matter effects, multi-parton dynamics, and potential BSM physics.

Method: Comprehensive review and comparative analysis of experimental data from CDF/D0 (Tevatron), Belle/BaBar/Belle II/BESIII/LEP (e+e−), and ALICE/ATLAS/CMS/LHCb (LHC Run 2 & early Run 3), covering differential production, polarization, spectroscopy, exotic state searches, heavy-ion suppression patterns, and multi-quarkonium correlations.

Result: Key findings include persistent J/ψ and ϒ polarization tensions with NRQCD; precise spectroscopic measurements revealing fully-heavy tetraquark candidates (e.g., X(6900), Zcs(4000)) and exotic structures in J/ψJ/ψ and J/ψψ(2S); sequential ϒ(nS) suppression in PbPb as a QGP thermometer; pPb data constraining cold nuclear matter effects; and evidence for multi-parton interactions via double-J/ψ production. LHCb’s observation of tetraquark candidates and Belle II/BESIII’s upcoming high-statistics runs mark major advances.

Conclusion: Heavy quarkonium remains a uniquely versatile probe of QCD across energy scales—from bound-state dynamics and factorization to deconfinement and hadronization—and offers growing sensitivity to BSM phenomena; however, theoretical challenges persist, especially in reconciling polarization data and rigorously predicting exotic state properties within NRQCD and lattice QCD.

Abstract: We review recent experimental advances in heavy quarkonium physics at the Tevatron, e+e- colliders (Belle, BaBar, Belle II, BESIII, and LEP), and the LHC (ALICE, ATLAS, CMS, and LHCb), including Run 2 and early Run 3 results. Differential cross sections and polarization measurements of charmonium (Jpsi, Psi(2S)) and bottomonium (Upsilon(nS)) test perturbative and non-perturbative QCD within the Non-Relativistic QCD (NRQCD) framework and probe symmetry relations among spin and orbital configurations. CDF and D0 established production baselines and exposed polarization tensions with early NRQCD predictions. Complementary e+e- measurements provide precise spectroscopy, branching fractions, radiative and hadronic transitions, and observations of exotic quarkonium-like states, constraining non-perturbative dynamics. LHC measurements extend production and polarization studies across broad kinematic ranges. Spectroscopic advances include fully-heavy tetraquark candidates, exotic structures in Jpsi Jpsi and Jpsi Psi(2S) reported by LHCb, and studies of toponium (ttbar) quasi-bound states. Sequential bottomonium suppression in PbPb collisions probes the quark-gluon plasma, while pPb measurements constrain cold nuclear matter effects. Multi-quarkonium production explores multi-parton interactions and exotic-hadron structure. Together, these results establish heavy quarkonium as a laboratory for QCD and a probe for physics beyond the Standard Model, while highlighting unresolved theoretical challenges. Future programs at the LHC, Belle II, and BESIII promise greater precision and new discoveries.

</details>


### [171] [Measurement of the $\lvert V_\mathrm{cb}\rvert$ element of the CKM matrix using $\mathrm{t\bar{t}}$ events in pp collisions at $\sqrt{s}$ = 13 TeV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01274)
*CMS Collaboration*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: A new measurement of |V_cb| is performed using tt̄ events in the lepton+jets channel at 13 TeV with 138 fb⁻¹ of CMS data; |V_cb| is extracted from the W→cb branching fraction using a multivariate discriminant based on b-tagging, yielding |V_cb| = (4.59⁺⁰·⁴⁴₋₀·₄₈)stat (⁺⁰·⁶₄₋₀·₇₆)syst × 10⁻² — a model-independent, electroweak-scale determination complementary to B-meson decay analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a complementary, electroweak-scale determination of |V_cb| that avoids nonperturbative QCD inputs required in traditional semileptonic B meson decay measurements.

Method: Analysis of tt̄ → (ℓν)(qq̄') events in the lepton+jets final state using 138 fb⁻¹ of √s = 13 TeV pp collision data from CMS; extraction of |V_cb| from the W → cb branching fraction via a multivariate discriminant primarily leveraging b-tagging information of jets assigned to the hadronic W decay.

Result: |V_cb| = [4.59⁺⁰·⁴⁴₋₀·₄₈ (stat) ⁺⁰·⁶₄₋₀·₇₆ (syst)] × 10⁻².

Conclusion: This result constitutes the first direct determination of |V_cb| from W → cb in top quark pair production, offering a QCD-model-independent constraint at the electroweak scale and serving as a valuable cross-check to inclusive and exclusive B-decay determinations.

Abstract: A measurement of the magnitude of the Cabibbo$-$Kobayashi$-$Maskawa matrix element $V_\mathrm{cb}$ is presented using top quark pair events produced in proton-proton collisions at $\sqrt{s}$ = 13 TeV. The data correspond to an integrated luminosity of 138 fb$^{-1}$ collected by the CMS experiment at the LHC. The measurement targets the lepton+jets final state, where one W boson decays leptonically and the other hadronically. The value of $\lvert V_\mathrm{cb}\rvert$ is extracted from the branching fraction of the W $\to$ cb decay. To identify this rare signal amid the dominant W $\to$ ud and W $\to$ cs backgrounds, a multivariate discriminant based primarily on the b jet tagging information of the jets assigned to the hadronic W boson decay is used. The measured value is $\lvert V_\mathrm{cb}\rvert$ = [4.59$^{+0.44}_{-0.48}$ (stat) $^{0.64}_{-0.76}$ (syst)] $\times$ 10$^{-2}$. This measurement provides a complementary determination of $\lvert V_\mathrm{cb}\rvert$ at the electroweak scale, independent of the nonperturbative quantum chromodynamics inputs used in measurements based on semileptonic B meson decays.

</details>


### [172] [World average charged-current inclusive neutrino cross section from 6 to 200 GeV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01435)
*Richard Gran*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper provides updated world-average inclusive charged-current muon neutrino and antineutrino cross section energy-dependence slopes (σ/Eν) across two energy ranges (6–30 GeV and 30–200 GeV), using PDG methodology; reports slight deviations at low energies, interprets them via nuclear effects and isoscalar corrections within the Bodek-Yang 2000 model and GENIE generators, and emphasizes their foundational role in structure function extraction, electroweak measurements, and neutrino oscillation analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To update and extend the world-average inclusive charged-current muon (anti)neutrino cross section slope σ/Eν with expanded experimental input, especially down to 6 GeV using the low-ν method, and to interpret observed energy-dependent deviations in terms of nuclear and isospin effects critical for precision neutrino physics.

Method: Applies Particle Data Group (PDG) averaging methodology to combine inclusive charged-current cross section data from multiple experiments; uses the low-ν method to anchor low-energy (6–30 GeV) measurements to the high-energy (30–200 GeV) average; interprets results using the Bodek-Yang 2000 model and two GENIE event generator configurations to assess nuclear effects and isoscalar corrections.

Result: High-energy (30–200 GeV): σ/Eν = (0.677 ± 0.013) × 10⁻³⁸ cm²/GeV (νμ), (0.335 ± 0.007) × 10⁻³⁸ cm²/GeV (ν̄μ); low-energy (6–30 GeV): (0.698 ± 0.018) × 10⁻³⁸ cm²/GeV (νμ), (0.316 ± 0.012) × 10⁻³⁸ cm²/GeV (ν̄μ); low-E values are marginally higher (νμ) and lower (ν̄μ) but consistent within uncertainties.

Conclusion: The updated σ/Eν values confirm consistency with prior standards at high energy, reveal subtle but physically interpretable energy dependence at low energy, and underscore the necessity of accurate nuclear and isoscalar modeling—especially in GENIE—for precision neutrino scattering predictions used in oscillation and electroweak analyses.

Abstract: The methods of the Particle Data Group are applied to produce a world average inclusive, charged-current, muon neutrino cross section energy-dependence slope $\sigma/E_\nu$ . For the energy range $30 < E_\nu < 200$, values obtained are (0.677 $\pm$ 0.013) and (0.335 $\pm$ 0.007) $\times 10^{-38}$ cm$^2$/GeV for neutrino and anti-neutrino, respectively. These values are practically identical to those in common use. This paper expands the experiments whose data are used and describes the evolution of measurements leading to the result. Next, the value for $\sigma/E_\nu$ is obtained for the low energy extensions by experiments that used the low-$\nu$ method to anchor to the high energy result. In the range $6 < E_\nu < 30$ GeV, values of (0.698 $\pm$ 0.018) and (0.316 $\pm$ 0.012) $\times 10^{-38}$ cm$^2$/GeV are obtained. These are higher than the high energy average for neutrino and lower for anti-neutrino, but consistent at the edge of the uncertainty estimates. An interpretation of the evolution of the inclusive cross section, nuclear effects, and isoscalar corrections is provided in the context of the Bodek Yang 2000 model and two configurations of the GENIE neutrino event generators. These cross sections underpin foundational neutrino quantities such as structure functions, electroweak parameter measurements, and oscillation parameter determinations.

</details>


### [173] [CELEBI: Design and Realization of a Reproducible Physics Analysis Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01788)
*Mingrui Zhao*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: Celebi is a framework for reproducible, evolving physics analyses, decoupling lightweight analysis definitions from execution and data products via an Analysis Repository, Production Factory, and Yuki middleware that generates Snakemake workflows; it introduces modular workflow architecture, structured file organization, and immutable 'impression'-based state preservation for provenance and long-term reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducibility in modern physics analysis is challenged by continuous evolution across code, configurations, parameters, datasets, and dependencies—requiring a coherent, persistent representation of both analysis logic and its provenance.

Method: Celebi employs a three-tier architecture: (1) an Analysis Repository storing lightweight, versioned analysis definitions; (2) a Production Factory managing execution and generated data; and (3) Yuki middleware that translates preserved analysis states into executable Snakemake workflows for heterogeneous runners. Its core methodological innovations are: modular workflow architecture, structured human-readable file organization (separating algorithms, configs, params, docs, deps), and the 'impression' mechanism—an immutable snapshotting system assigning persistent identifiers to analysis states and binding results unambiguously to their origins.

Result: Celebi enables robust reproducibility, precise provenance tracking, collaborative development, and long-term preservation of physics analyses—demonstrated through its decoupled, backend-agnostic design and support for immutable state anchoring of results.

Conclusion: Celebi establishes a principled, scalable framework for managing evolving physics analyses by rigorously separating analysis definition from execution and data, thereby fulfilling key requirements for scientific reproducibility and archival integrity in high-energy and computational physics.

Abstract: Reproducibility in modern physics analysis requires more than preserving source code or workflow descriptions. Analyses evolve through changes in code, configurations, parameters, input datasets, and dependencies, requiring a coherent representation of both the analysis and the provenance of its results. We present Celebi, a framework for organizing and preserving evolving physics analyses while keeping analysis definitions lightweight and separate from generated data products. Celebi maintains analysis definitions and preserved states in an Analysis Repository, while execution and generated results are managed separately through a Production Factory. The middleware layer, Yuki, connects preserved analysis states to execution infrastructure by constructing executable Snakemake workflows and dispatching them to supported runners, keeping the analysis representation independent of execution backends. This paper presents three main design concepts. First, the workflow architecture provides a modular representation of analysis components and their dependencies. Second, a structured file organization realizes this model in a human-readable form, separating reusable algorithms, task-specific configurations, parameters, documentation, and dependencies into well-defined objects. Third, the impression mechanism preserves immutable states of these objects and their dependencies, assigning persistent identities to specific analysis configurations and linking generated results unambiguously to their originating states. Together, these concepts establish a coherent representation of evolving physics analyses, supporting reproducibility, provenance tracking, collaboration, and long-term preservation.

</details>


### [174] [CELEBI: Towards Reproducible and FAIR Physics Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01804)
*Mingrui Zhao*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: Celebi is an analysis-management framework for modern physics that preserves provenance by linking evolving analysis components (algorithms, tasks, dependencies) to immutable 'impressions' of states and their corresponding results, enabling reproducibility, collaboration, systematic variation, and transparent review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the growing difficulty in reproducing, tracing, and sharing physics analyses due to continuous evolution of datasets, selections, software, and strategies over time.

Method: Celebi introduces a structured workflow model organizing reusable algorithms and tasks with explicit dependencies; implements 'immutable impressions' to snapshot analysis states; associates retained results with their exact generating states; and enables controlled rerunning and side-by-side inspection of results and provenance.

Result: A functional analysis-management framework that supports reproducible execution, state-preserving provenance capture, collaborative development, and retrospective inspection/comparison of results with their generating conditions—demonstrated in real-world physics analysis contexts.

Conclusion: Celebi provides a practical, integrated solution for analysis lifecycle management, significantly improving transparency, reusability, and reliability in high-energy and nuclear physics analyses without disrupting ongoing development workflows.

Abstract: Modern physics analyses evolve continuously as datasets, selections, parameters, software environments, and analysis strategies are updated. Over the lifetime of an analysis, this evolution can make it difficult to determine the conditions under which an earlier result was produced or to transfer the analysis reliably between collaborators. We present Celebi, an analysis-management framework that maintains a persistent connection between evolving analysis materials and their generated results. Celebi organizes reusable algorithms, tasks, and their dependencies into a structured workflow, preserves selected states as immutable impressions, and associates retained results with the states responsible for their production. This organization supports analysis modification, systematic variations, collaboration, and review while the working analysis continues to evolve. Celebi supports controlled rerunning of preserved analysis states. When results remain retained, they can be inspected and compared together with their corresponding states. By capturing provenance during everyday analysis development, Celebi reduces the need to reconstruct analysis history retrospectively and provides a practical foundation for more transparent and reusable physics analysis.

</details>


### [175] [Search for supersymmetric particle pair production in final states with two oppositely charged leptons and large missing transverse momentum in proton-proton collisions at $\sqrt{s}$ = 13 TeV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02183)
*CMS Collaboration*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: A CMS search at 13 TeV with 138 fb⁻¹ finds no SUSY signal in opposite-sign dilepton + MET final states; sets mass limits on charginos (up to 1120 GeV), top squarks (up to 540 GeV), and sleptons (up to 700 GeV) under R-parity–conserving simplified models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test supersymmetry (SUSY) via direct production of SUSY particle pairs—specifically charginos, top squarks, and sleptons—in dilepton + missing transverse momentum signatures, motivated by naturalness, dark matter candidates (LSP neutralino), and gaps in SM coverage.

Method: Search for pp → χ̃⁺χ̃⁻, t̃t̃*, ℓ̃ℓ̃* → ℓ⁺ℓ⁻ + E_T^miss in CMS data at √s = 13 TeV (138 fb⁻¹); uses background estimation from data-driven and simulation-based methods; interprets results in R-parity–conserving simplified models with χ̃⁰₁ as LSP.

Result: No significant excess observed over SM prediction; exclusion limits set: chargino masses ≤ 1120 GeV (for χ̃⁰₁ ≤ 490 GeV), top squark masses ≤ 500–540 GeV (for m_t̃ − m_χ̃⁰₁ ∈ [m_W, m_t]), slepton masses ≤ 700 GeV (for m_χ̃⁰₁ = 0).

Conclusion: No evidence for direct SUSY particle pair production in the studied channel; results significantly constrain parameter space of simplified SUSY models, particularly for electroweakinos and third-generation squarks/sleptons.

Abstract: A search is presented for direct production of pairs of supersymmetric particles in final states with a pair of oppositely charged leptons (electrons or muons) and missing transverse momentum. The analysis uses proton-proton collision data at $\sqrt{s}$ = 13 TeV, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 138 fb$^{-1}$, recorded with the CMS detector at the CERN LHC. The observed data do not exhibit any excess over the standard model predictions. Constraints are derived on chargino, top squark, and slepton pair production in the context of simplified models with $R$-parity conservation. In these models, the neutralino is taken to be the lightest supersymmetric particle. For chargino production, an exclusion region in the chargino-neutralino mass plane is determined, which extends to chargino masses of 1120 GeV and neutralino masses of 490 GeV. For mass splittings between the lighter top squark and the neutralino ranging from the W boson mass to the top quark mass, top squark masses up to 500$-$540 GeV are excluded. Slepton masses are excluded up to 700 GeV for a massless neutralino.

</details>


### [176] [Experimental Searches for Muon to Electron Conversion in Nuclei](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02579)
*Yoshitaka Kuno*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reviews experimental efforts to detect charged lepton flavor violation via muon-to-electron conversion in nuclei (μ⁻ → e⁻), emphasizing its sensitivity to beyond-Standard-Model physics, experimental techniques, background suppression, current status (e.g., COMET, Mu2e), and prospects for future experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Charged lepton flavor violation (CLFV), particularly μ⁻ → e⁻ conversion in nuclei, is a highly sensitive probe of physics beyond the Standard Model due to its negligible SM background and strong dependence on new physics scales.

Method: Experimental detection of μ⁻ → e⁻ conversion using stopped muons on nuclear targets; relies on identifying monoenergetic electrons at 105 MeV (for aluminum), precise beam timing, active vetoing, and background suppression via particle identification and shielding.

Result: No signal observed to date; current best upper limit on conversion rate (Al) is ~7 × 10⁻¹³ (SINDRUM II); next-generation experiments (COMET Phase-I, Mu2e) aim for sensitivities of ~few × 10⁻¹⁷, improving by four orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: μ⁻ → e⁻ conversion remains one of the most promising CLFV channels for discovering new physics; observation would require cross-checks across target nuclei and complementary processes (e.g., μ → eγ, μ → 3e) to disentangle underlying effective operators or UV models.

Abstract: Charged lepton flavor violation (CLFV) provides a highly sensitive probe of physics beyond the Standard Model. This experimental review discusses $\mu^{-} \to e^{-}$ conversion in nuclei, which is a muon CLFV process characterized by a clean monoenergetic-electron signature and the ability to exploit large samples of stopped muons. It describes the physics motivation, basic experimental principles, key background sources, and search strategies, as well as the status and prospects of past and next-generation experiments such as COMET and Mu2e. Future experimental concepts may extend the sensitivity even further. If a signal is observed, comparisons among results obtained with different target nuclei and with those from complementary searches for other muon CLFV decay modes will be essential for identifying the underlying interaction.

</details>


### [177] [Search for dark photon dark matter near $\mathbf{1\,meV}$ using a superconducting mixer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02734)
*K. Hanimura,S. Adachi,Y. Hasegawa,S. Honda,T. Nakajima,H. Ogawa,J. Suzuki,O. Tajima,H. Takeuchi*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reports a direct search for dark photon dark matter (DPDM) in the ~1 meV mass range using a superconducting mixer-based cryogenic millimeter-wave receiver; no signal was detected, yielding 95% CL upper limits on kinetic mixing χ < (1.6–1.9)×10⁻⁹, competitive with solar DP bounds, and outlines plans to extend the search to 0.7–1.1 meV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To directly detect dark photon dark matter (DPDM) in the meV mass range—where DPDM can resonantly convert to photons at metal surfaces—using high-sensitivity, low-noise millimeter-wave detection techniques not previously applied to this parameter space.

Method: Used a cryogenic millimeter-wave receiver with a superconducting mixer to perform narrowband spectroscopic search for conversion photons from DPDM incident on a metal plate; targeted a 2.0 peV (0.000002 meV) mass window centered at 1.0047588 meV (242.9496 GHz), integrating ~1 hour of data.

Result: No statistically significant excess observed; derived 95% confidence level upper limits on kinetic mixing: χ < (1.6–1.9)×10⁻⁹ over the searched mass range; limits are comparable to existing constraints from solar dark photon searches.

Conclusion: Superconducting mixer-based direct detection is a viable and competitive technique for meV-scale DPDM; the approach enables pathfinding for broader, higher-sensitivity searches across 0.7–1.1 meV (170–260 GHz).

Abstract: We demonstrate the use of a superconducting mixer for direct searches for dark photon dark matter (DPDM) in the meV mass range. DPDM couples to the ordinary electromagnetic field through kinetic mixing with a coupling constant $\chi$ and can convert into ordinary photons at the surface of a metal plate. For DPDM masses of 1 meV, the conversion photons have frequencies of around 240 GHz. At such frequencies, superconducting mixers enable low-noise spectroscopic measurements. We performed a direct search for DPDM in the mass range 1.0047578-1.0047598 meV, corresponding to the frequency range 242.94935-242.94983 GHz, using approximately one hour of data obtained with a cryogenic millimeter-wave receiver based on a superconducting mixer. No significant excess was found, and we set upper limits of $\chi < (1.6\text{-}1.9)\times10^{-9}$ at the 95% confidence level. These limits are comparable to existing constraints from solar dark photons. By applying this method, we plan to extend the search to the wider mass range of 0.7-1.1 meV, corresponding to 170-260 GHz, with improved sensitivity.

</details>


### [178] [Study of the up-down asymmetry in $B^+\to K^+π^+π^-γ$ using $K_1(1270)$ amplitudes constrained by semileptonic $D$ Decays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03035)
*Yijia Zeng,Hailong Ma*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper estimates the up-down asymmetry in B+→K+π+π−γ using K1(1270)→Kππ amplitude models constrained by D+(0)→K−π+π0(−)e+νe semileptonic decays; isolated K1(1270) models underpredict the measured asymmetry (0.092±0.023), but interference with the K1(1400) tail reduces the tension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the measured up-down asymmetry H_ud in B+→K+π+π−γ, which may probe new physics or require refined hadronic modeling, particularly in the K1(1270) resonance region where nonresonant and overlapping contributions complicate interpretation.

Method: Constructs K1(1270)→Kππ amplitude models constrained by experimental Dalitz plot analyses of charmed-meson semileptonic decays D+(0)→K−π+π0(−)e+νe; computes the up-down asymmetry H_ud in B+→K+π+π−γ within M(K+π+π−)∈[1.1,1.3] GeV/c²; assesses model dependence by varying K1(1270) parameterizations and includes K1(1400) tail interference.

Result: Isolated K1(1270)-based models systematically yield H_ud < 0.092 (by ~1–2σ depending on model), assuming λ_γ = 1; inclusion of K1(1400) tail interference improves agreement, reducing tension with data.

Conclusion: The discrepancy between model predictions and measurement suggests either limitations in pure K1(1270) descriptions or sensitivity to higher-mass resonant contributions (e.g., K1(1400)); improved amplitude models incorporating coupled-channel or dispersive constraints are needed for robust SM prediction and NP discrimination.

Abstract: Using $K_1(1270)\to K\pi\pi$ amplitude models constrained by the charmed-meson semileptonic decays $D^{+(0)}\to K^-\pi^+\pi^{0(-)} e^+\nu_e$, we present a model-dependent estimate of the up-down asymmetry observed in $B^+\to K^+\pi^+\pi^-\gamma$ in the mass region $M_{K^+\pi^+\pi^-}\in[1.1,1.3]$~GeV/$c^2$. Estimates based on an isolated $K_1(1270)$ show significant model dependence, but systematically lie below the measured asymmetry $\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{ud}}=(0.092\pm0.023)$ when assuming $\lambda_\gamma=1$. We also explore interference with the $K_1(1400)$ tail and find that it can appreciably reduce the tension.

</details>
